2.       RECOGNITION  
 
A.  Troy School District’s Outstanding Teacher of the Year Awards 
 
B.  Mini Grant Awards – Michigan Schools & Government Credit 
Union  
 
 
This evening we are pleased to recognize our Teacher of the Year 
nominees and recipients and those who received mini grant awards 
from the Michigan Schools and Government Credit Union.  This 
evening, we had a short reception prior to the Board meeting to 
celebrate these accomplishments.  I would like to thank the credit 
union for sponsoring the welcome reception for the teachers prior to 
this evening’s meeting. 
 
At this time, I would like to introduce our director of community 
relations, Tim McAvoy, to begin our recognition program.  Following 
our recognition of the Teacher of the Year nominees, Sally Gibson, vice 
president of business development for the credit union, will recognize 
the mini grant recipients.

BOARD MEETING   MINUTES  MARCH 2, 2010 
    SERVICES BUILDING  
 
  
President Underwood called the meeting to order at 7:00 
p.m. 
 
In addition to Ms. Underwood, present were Board 
members Edmunds, Fleming, Hauff, Miletti, Philippar t, 
and Pochodylo.  Also present were Superintendent 
Fowler; administrators Adamczyk, Machesky, McAvoy, 
Mull, Stromayer, Bryan, Miller, Kwasny, Cottone, Ke eling, 
and Witt; and approximately 70 additional members o f 
the audience. 
 
RECOGNITION 
Senator John Pappageorge 
and Representative Marty 
Knollenberg 
10-018 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
It was moved by Mrs. Edmunds and supported by Mrs. 
Fleming that: 
 
Whereas , Governor Granholm signed a school aid bill on 
October 19, 2009 that included a line item veto of 20j 
funding for the Troy School District and students i n 40 
other Michigan communities; and 
 
Whereas , this action arbitrarily cut funding to the 
students in Troy by nearly $3 million annually, or $248.18 
per pupil, beyond the reductions that were already 
targeted to our community and every Michigan school  
district; and 
 
Whereas , this veto broke the promise made to Troy 
voters as part of Proposal A in 1994 that their com munity 
would be held harmless in efforts to address school  
funding inequities; and 
 
Whereas , both State Senator John Pappageorge and 
State Representative Marty Knollenberg have authore d 
and voted for measures that would return these crit ical 
funds to the Troy School District community; and  
 
Whereas , both State Senator Pappageorge and State 
Representative Knollenberg continue to work tireles sly in 
Lansing to advocate for the reinstatement of 20j fu nds; 
and 
 
Be it therefore resolved that the Troy Board of 
Education extends its sincere appreciation to State  
Senator Pappageorge and State Representative 
5. A.     03.16.10  
 
7
Troy Board of Education Meeting 2 March 2, 2010 
 
Knollenberg for their continued efforts to support Troy 
children by advocating for fair and necessary resou rces 
needed in our schools; and  
 
Let it further be resolved  that, in addition to extending 
our appreciation, the Troy Board of Education urges  both 
State Senator Pappageorge and State Representative 
Knollenberg to vote against any school aid bill tha t does 
not include a full reinstatement of the 20j revenue  for the 
students of the Troy School District and other 20j 
communities.   
 
Yes: 7 
No: 0

Yes: 7 
No: 0 
 
It was moved by Mr. Miletti and supported by Mrs. 
Philippart that: 
 
Whereas  the electorate of the State of Michigan passed 
an amendment in November 1978 to the State’s 
Constitution that required the State to fund mandat es 
imposed on local units of government (often referre d to 
as the “Headlee Amendment”); and 
  
Whereas  the Headlee Amendment (Article IX, Section 
29) states: 
 
“The State is hereby prohibited from reducing the 
State financed proportion of the necessary costs of  
any existing activity or service required of units of 
Local Government by State law. A new activity or 
service or an increase in the level of any activity  or 
service beyond that required by existing law shall 
not be required by the legislature or any State 
agency of units of local government, unless a State  
appropriation is made and disbursed to pay the unit 
of local government for any necessary increased 
costs. The provision of this section shall not appl y 
to costs incurred pursuant to Article VI, Section 
18.”  And 
 
Whereas  the Headlee Amendment became effective on 
December 23, 1978; and 
 
9
Troy Board of Education Meeting 4 March 2, 2010 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
Whereas  the State Legislature established the Legislative 
Commission on Statutory Mandates (LCSM) through P.A . 
98 of 2007, as amended by P.A. 356 of 2008 and 
assigned the LCSM to identify mandates (including t hose 
involving reports) and the related cost of the mand ates to 
local units of government, along with recommendatio ns to 
resolve the unfunded mandates; and 
 
Whereas  the LCSM worked with the Citizens Research 
Council (issued an analysis of other states’ statut es and 
constitutional requirements similar to the Headlee 
Amendment) and local units of government associatio ns, 
including the Michigan School Business Officials an d 
Michigan Association of School Administrators; and 
 
Whereas  the LCSM issued a report in June 2009 entitled 
“Interim Report of the Legislative Commission on 
Statutory Mandates” that indicated, among other mat ters, 
that the State had failed to enact legislation enab ling the 
Headlee Amendment and has not complied with the 
Headlee Amendment since its adoption in 1978; and 
 
Whereas the LCSM completed its report in December 
2009 entitled “Final Report of the Legislative Comm ission 
on Statutory Mandates” that reaffirms the Interim R eport 
results and provides recommendations, including, bu t not 
limited to: 
 
• Drafted legislation and court rules that would 
mitigate unfunded mandates imposed on local 
units of government in the future. 
• Proposed procedures that will prevent new 
unfunded mandates from being imposed on 
local units of government. 
• Proposed procedures that would be corrective 
should unfunded mandates be imposed that 
include, among other requirements: 
 
o A submission of an action before the 
Court of Appeals to be heard by a special 
master in order to rule on whether the 
matter is a mandate and if the mandate is 
underfunded. 
o Require the Court of Appeals to rule on 
the above within six months of the filing. 
o Should the Court of Appeals not rule on 
the above within six months, the local 
unit of government would have no 
obligation to continue to provide the 
services until such time as the State 
complies with the Headlee Amendment. 
 
Be it therefore resolved that the Troy School District 
10
Troy Board of Education Meeting 5 March 2, 2010 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
Budget Reduction Plan for 
2010/2011 School Year 
 
Authorization to Implement 
Budget Reduction Plan 
10-025 
Board of Education supports the findings and 
recommendations in the interim and final reports of  the 
Legislative Commission on Statutory Mandates and 
encourages the Governor, Legislature and Supreme Co urt 
to adopt and enact the recommendations cited in the  final 
report. 
 
Be it further resolved  that the Troy School District 
Board of Education approves the release of this res olution 
to be distributed to the Governor, Legislators and 
Supreme Court. 
 
Yes: 7 
No: 0 
 
Mr. Adamczyk gave a report on the proposed budget 
reduction plan for the 2010/2011 school year. 
 
It was moved by Mrs. Pochodylo and supported by Mr.  
Hauff that be it therefore resolved  that the Troy School 
District Board of Education hereby authorizes the 
administration to begin taking the necessary action s to 
implement approximately $6 million in budget 
adjustments and draw on existing fund equity to add ress 
the projected deficit in the 2010-2011 school year. 
 
Therefore  be it further resolved  that the Board of 
Education will continue to work with its community,  
parents, staff, bargaining units and state lawmaker s to 
continue to advocate for our students and the resou rces 
necessary to maintain and enhance our exemplary 
programs. 
 
Yes: 7

BOARD OF EDUCATION 
TROY, MICHIGAN 
 
BOARD WORKSHOP   MINUTES  MARCH 9, 2010 
    ADMINISTRATIVE CENTER  
 
President Underwood called the board workshop to order at 6:10 p.m.  In addition 
to Ms. Underwood, the following Board members were present:  Mrs. Edmunds, 
Mrs. Fleming, Mr. Hauff, Mr. Miletti, Mrs. Philippart and Mrs. Pochodylo.  Also in 
attendance were Superintendent Fowler, administrators Adamczyk, Hannon, 
Machesky, McAvoy, Mull, Stromayer, and Witt.  In addition, three community 
member was present. 
 
The following items were discussed: 
 
1. Public Communication* 
 Mr. Bruce Bloomingdale addressed the Board about t he budget process. 
 
2. Personal Curriculum 
 
3. Graduation Requirements 
 
4. New and Revised Board Policies 
a.  B-1208, Voting 
b.  B-1211, Public Participation in Board of Education Meetings 
c.  B-1301, Agenda Format for Regular Meetings 
d.  C-1402, Smoking Policy 
e.  D-1106, Fixed Assets (NEW) 
f.  K-1303, Political and/or Commercial Advertising by District Employees  
g.  K-1304, Advertising on School District Property and in School District 
Electronic Media 
 
5. Status of 1000-Day Plans  
 
6. Other 
 
The superintendent and her staff left the meeting at 8:50 p.m. and the Board 
discussed the process for the superintendent’s evaluation and Board self-
assessment survey results. 
 
The meeting was adjourned at 10:05 p.m. 
      
 Respectfully submitted, 
 
 
 
 Nancy L. Philippart, Secretary

SOFTWARE LICENSES 
 
 Invitations to bid were sent to 6 potential bidders  with 3 
responding (none of which were “No Bid”).  The resp onses 
were reviewed with Mr. Scott Bryan, Director of 
Technology, and it is recommended the bid be awarde d as 
stated below.

SUGGESTED RESOLUTION

 Supported by:

software licenses be awarded to Networks Group in the amount

MEMORANDUM  
 
To: Mike Adamczyk, Assistant Superintendent, Business Services 
From: Scott Bryan, Director of Technology 
Date: March 16, 2010 
Re: Anti-Virus software license purchase 
Cc: Frank Lams, Purchasing Supervisor 
 
On March 1, 2010, the Technology Department issued a bid for financing the renewal of software licenses 
for Trend Micro anti-virus products for five years, to protect district desktops, servers, storage, and email 
systems.  Bids were opened on March 15. 
 
Three bids were received, with two being identically priced at $119,273.85 for the five year term.  Upon 
negotiation with both low bid vendors, a further discount was extended to the District to purchase outright 
instead of financing.  An equal 10% discount was offered by each of the low bidders, for a total of 
$107,346.47.  As Frank Lams and I discussed with you, the District is permitted by law to consider 
companies with headquarters in Michigan as a determining factor for selecting a bidder.  Based on this and 
on positive reference checks of the bidder, I recommend that this project be awarded to Networks Group, 
Inc., in the amount of $107,346.47.  I have attached a copy of their updated offer to this memo. 
 
This item requires Board action due to the dollar amount of the purchase; please place this on the March 16, 
2010 agenda.  Thank you for your attention to this matter. 
Barbara A. Fowler, Ph.D., Superintendent of Schools

7.   CURRICULUM       
 
 
 A. Elementary Reading Program  
 
The elementary reading program has undergone extensive review and 
revision, beginning in the 2006/07 school year.  As we approach full 
implementation of the revised reading instructional program in grades 
kindergarten through 5 th , we would like to take this opportunity to 
share the process and changes in elementary reading instruction.  The 
committee was co-chaired by principals Janice Brzezinski from Hill and 
Lois Byrne from Martell and teachers Dr. Karen Reese from Morse and 
Trudi Motzenbecker from Wattles.  Dr. Reese and Mrs. Motzenbecker 
will make the presentation tonight to share the revisions developed by 
a large committee of teachers working collaboratively during the last 
three years.  
 
_________________________________________________________________ 
Recommended Action:  Introduce Dr. Reese and Mrs. Motzenbecker

DRAFT     TROY SCHOOL DISTRICT READING CURRICULUM UNITS OF STUDY    DRAFT  
196
DRAFT DRAFT DRAFT DRAFT Reader Profile for Grade One (Levels D/4-E/7) DRAFT DRAFT DRAFT DRAFT  
8/18/09 Troy School District Reading Committee                                                             1 
 
Self -Monitoring   
Students will… 
 
1.  Track print with eyes and use finger only at point of difficulty, left to 
right. 
 
2.  Know a core of high-frequency words (18-30) that can be read 
automatically and can be used to self-monitor, using word parts and letter-
sound information. 
 
3.  Read familiar texts with fluency, phrasing and at least 98% accuracy. 
 
 
4.  Monitor for all sources of information during reading:  checking to make 
sure what has been read makes sense, sounds right and looks right. 
 
 
5.  Reread to problem solve, correct, or confirm. 
 
 
 
6.  Reread to search for information. 
 
7.  Recognize and use word parts (onsets and rimes) to solve words while 
reading 
 
 
 
 
 
“I Can” Statements  
 
 
1.  I can follow the words with my eyes and use my finger 
when I get to a word I don’t know. 
 
2.  I can read many high-frequency words quickly and read a 
new word by pointing and sliding my index finger through 
the word blending the sounds of the letters I know. 
 
3.  I can read familiar books like I’m talking, with good 
phrasing and fluency. 
 
4.  I can monitor my reading to make sure what I have read 
makes sense, sounds right, and looks right. 
 
 
5.  I can recognize an error I make when I am reading and I 
go back, slow down, and correct my error so that it 
makes sense.  
 
6.  I can reread to search for information. 
 
7.  I can recognize and use word families to decode unknown 
words. 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
197
DRAFT DRAFT DRAFT DRAFT Reader Profile for Grade One (Levels D/4-E/7) DRAFT DRAFT DRAFT DRAFT  
8/18/09 Troy School District Reading Committee                                                             2 
Comprehension -Thinking within the text  
Students will… 
 
1.  Notice details in pictures and use information to understand the text.  
 
 
2.  Notice, search for, remember, and discuss information that is important to 
understanding.  
 
3.  Recall a series of events in order.  
 
4.  Provide an oral summary of a text with appropriate details.  
 
 
5.  Reflect punctuation through appropriate pausing and intonation while 
reading orally.  
 
6.  Reread to solve words or think about ideas and resume good rate of 
reading. 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
“I Can” Statements  
 
 
1.  I can describe illustrations and compare and contrast my 
interpretations to the text. 
 
2.  I can look for, remember, and talk about important 
information while I am reading. 
 
3.  I can remember the sequence of events in the story. 
 
4.  I can use the text structure (characters, setting, 
problem, and solution) to summarize the story. 
 
5.  I can pause or change my voice while reading to show 
that I understand punctuation. 
 
6.  When I come to a word I don’t know, I can solve it by 
rereading or thinking, and then begin reading again. 
 
 
 
  
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
198
DRAFT DRAFT DRAFT DRAFT Reader Profile for Grade One (Levels D/4-E/7) DRAFT DRAFT DRAFT DRAFT  
8/18/09 Troy School District Reading Committee                                                             3 
Comprehension -Thinking beyond the text  
Students will… 
 
1.  Begin to make connections, ask questions and make predictions before, 
during, and after reading. 
 
 
2.  Show words in the text of new ideas and information. 
 
3.  Infer and talk about characters’ feelings, motives, and attributes. 
 
 
4.  Infer and talk about causes for feelings, motives, or actions. 
 
 
5.  Show evidence in the print or pictures to support the inference. 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
“I Can” Statements  
 
 
1.  I can begin to makes text-self connections, ask 
questions, and make predictions, before, during, and 
after reading. 
 
2.  I can find new words in the text that show new ideas. 
 
3.  I can share my thinking about characters’ feelings and 
motives. 
 
4.  I can share my thinking about why characters behave and 
act in certain ways throughout the story. 
 
5.  I can show you the words or illustrations in the story 
that support my thinking. 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
199
DRAFT DRAFT DRAFT DRAFT Reader Profile for Grade One (Levels D/4-E/7) DRAFT DRAFT DRAFT DRAFT  
8/18/09 Troy School District Reading Committee                                                             4 
Comprehension - Thinking about the text  
Students will… 
 
1.  Recognize whether a text is fiction or nonfiction.  
 
 
2.  Recognize and discuss how print layout or features are used to reflect 
meaning (such as large or bold words).  
 
3.  Understand that a story has a beginning, a series of events, and an end.  
 
 
4.  Understand how writers use interesting characters and situations.  
 
 
5.  Share opinions about the text as a whole (beginning, characters, ending). 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
“I Can” Statements  
 
 
1.  After looking through a text, I can tell you whether it is 
fiction or non-fiction. 
 
2.  I can use titles, headings, and large or bold words to help 
me read. 
 
3.  I know that a fiction story has a beginning, a sequence of 
events, and an ending. 
 
4.  I can share my thinking about how writers use 
interesting characters and situations. 
 
5.  I can share my opinion about the text.  
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
200
DRAFT DRAFT DRAFT DRAFT Reader Profile for Grade One (Levels D/4-E/7) DRAFT DRAFT DRAFT DRAFT  
8/18/09 Troy School District Reading Committee                                                             5 
       Word Study  
Students will…. 
 
1.  Build words families such as 
play-plays, play-playing.  
 
2.  Know the difference between a vowel (V) and a consonant (C). 
 
 
3.  Explore consonant sounds represented by consonant diagraphs at the 
beginning and end of a word (sh, ch, th, ph, and wh). 
 
4.  Explore consonant clusters that blend two consonant sounds (bl, cl, gl, fl, pl, 
pr, br, dr, gr, tr, cr, fr, sl, sn, sp, st, sw, sc, sk, sm,). 
 
5.  Recognize that vowels have two sounds (short, long). 
 
6.  Recognize and use short vowel sounds in words. 
 
7.  Understand that every syllable has a vowel.  
 
8.  Know some long vowel patterns- when an “e” is at the end of the word the 
vowel is long (CVCe). 
 
9.  Explore word families consisting of two letters (an, am, at, ap, ad, in, ip, ig, 
it, ug, ut, og, ot, op, ed, et, en). 
 
10.  Recognize and use –ing at the end of words. 
 
11.  Understand the concept of plural. 
 
12.  Understand that changing the beginning or the end of a word can make a 
new word. 
“I Can” Statements  
 
 
1.  I can build word families with endings. 
 
2.  I can know the difference between a vowel and a 
consonant. 
 
3.  I can explore consonant diagraphs at the beginning and 
end of words. 
 
4.  I can explore consonant clusters that blend two 
consonant sounds.  
 
5.  I can explain that vowels have two sounds. 
 
6.  I can recognize and use short vowel sounds in words. 
 
7.  I can understand that every syllable has a vowel. 
 
8.  I can demonstrate that I know the ‘magic e’ rule in some 
words.. 
 
9.  I can explore word families with two letters. 
 
 
10.  I can notice and use –ing at the end of words. 
 
11.  I can show I understand plurals. 
 
12.  I can show that I understand that placing a new letter at 
the beginning or end of a word makes a new word. 
 
 
201
DRAFT DRAFT DRAFT DRAFT  Reader Profile for Grade Five (Level U/50) DRAFT DRAFT DRAFT DRAFT  
8/18/09  Troy School District Reading Committee                                                               1 
Self -Monitoring   
Students will… 
 
1.  Demonstrate ability to use, automatically and flexibly, a wide range of 
decoding strategies (for example, dividing words into syllables, using 
phonograms within multi syllable words, using words parts, using 
prefixed and affixes, and connecting words to known words). 
(R.WS.05.05) (R.WS.05.02) 
 
2.  Use word-solving strategies, background knowledge, analogies, graphics, 
text context, and readers’ tools (glossaries, dictionaries, and electronic 
devices) to solve words, including content-specific and technical words. 
(R.WS.05.07) 
 
 
 
 
3.  Continue to monitor accuracy and understanding, self-correcting when 
errors detract from meaning.  
 
 
 
4.  Automatically recognize frequently encountered words (Dolch list-first 
1000) in print with the number of words that can be read fluently 
increasing steadily throughout the year. (R.WS.05.03)  
 
5.  Fluently read beginning grade-level text and increasingly demanding text 
as the year progresses. (R.WS.05.06) 
 
6.  Change style and pace of reading to reflect purpose. 
 
 
 
“I Can” Statements  
 
 
1.  I can read unfamiliar words by: 
a.  dividing words into syllables 
b.  using base and root words 
c.  using prefixes and affixes 
d.  connecting new words to words I already know 
 
2.  I can help myself understand the meaning of words by using: 
a.  graphics 
b.  prior knowledge 
c.  analogies 
d.  context clues 
e.  dictionaries, glossaries, and computers 
 
 
3.  When what I read does not make sense I can: 
a.  reread a phrase or sentence 
b.  make a connection 
c.  use context clues 
 
4.  I can read the first 1000 Dolch words automatically. 
 
 
 
5.  I can read Level T books and increase my reading to Level V 
or above. 
 
6.  I can adjust how fast I read depending on what and why I am 
reading.  
 
 
 
 
202
DRAFT DRAFT DRAFT DRAFT  Reader Profile for Grade Five (Level U/50) DRAFT DRAFT DRAFT DRAFT  
8/18/09  Troy School District Reading Committee                                                               2 
Comprehension -Thinking within the text  
Students will… 
1.  Explain when to use and apply word structure, sentence structure and 
prediction to aid in decoding words and understanding meanings of words 
encountered in context. (R.WS.05.01) 
 
2.  Reread to solve words or think about ideas and resume good rate of 
reading. 
 
3.  Search for and use information in a wide range of graphics and integrate 
with information from print (for example, pictures, captions, diagrams, 
illustrations with labels, maps, charts). (R.CM.05.04) 
 
 
 
4.  Use a full range of readers’ tools to search for information (table of 
contents, glossary, heading and subheadings, call-outs, pronunciation 
guides, index, references). (R.IT.05.03) 
 
 
 
5.  Analyze how characters’ traits and setting define plot, climax, the role 
of dialogue and how problems are resolved.  (R.NT.05.03) 
 
6.  Apply significant knowledge from grade-level science, social studies, and 
mathematics texts. (R.CM.05.04) 
 
7.  Identify important ideas and information (longer texts with chapters 
and sometimes multiple texts) and organize them in a concise summary in 
order to remember and use them as background knowledge in reading or 
for discussion and writing. (R.CM.05.02) 
 
8.  Self monitor comprehension when reading or listening by questioning, 
rereading, or listening again if uncertain about meaning, inferring, 
summarizing, and engaging in discussion. (R.MT.05.01) 
“I Can” Statements  
 
1.  I can explain how I use word structure, sentence structure, 
and prediction when reading and understanding new words 
while reading. 
 
2.  I can reread to solve words or think about ideas and begin 
reading again. 
 
3.  To better understand what I am reading I can use: 
a.  pictures and illustrations with labels 
b.  captions and diagrams 
c.  maps, charts, graphs, timelines 
d.  introductions and conclusions 
 
4.  To better understand what I am reading I use: 
a.  table of contents, glossary, indexes 
b.  headings and subheadings 
c.  call-outs and pronunciation guides 
d.  references to search for information 
 
5.  I can tell you how a character’s traits and setting affects 
the plot, climax, dialogue, problem and solution of the story. 
 
6.  I can use what I have learned from my math, social studies, 
and science books. 
 
7.  I can identify important information from non-fiction and 
fiction books to use in discussions, writing, and reading. 
 
 
 
8.  When what I read doesn’t make sense, I can: 
       Reread, make connections, use context clues, ask   
       questions,  infer, summarize, or talk with others.

DRAFT DRAFT DRAFT DRAFT  Reader Profile for Grade Five (Level U/50) DRAFT DRAFT DRAFT DRAFT  
8/18/09  Troy School District Reading Committee                                                               3 
Comprehension -Thinking beyond the text  
Students will… 
 
1.  Make and continually revise a wide range of predictions (what 
characters will do, what will happen to solve the problem) based on 
personal experiences, content knowledge, and knowledge of similar 
texts. (R.MT.05.01) 
 
2.  Use knowledge from personal experiences in the interpretation of 
characters and events, particularly content, situation, and themes in oral 
and written responses. (R.CM.05.01) 
 
3.  Analyze global themes, universal truths, and principles within and across 
texts to create a deeper understanding by drawing conclusions, making 
inferences, and synthesizing. (R.CM.05.03) 
 
4.  Mentally form categories of related information and revise them as new 
information is acquired across the text. (R.MT.05.01) 
 
5.  Integrate existing content knowledge with new information from a text 
to consciously create new understanding. 
 
6.  In texts with multiple complex characters, infer traits, motivations, and 
changes through examining how the writer describes them, what they 
do, what they say and think, and what other characters say about them. 
(R.NT.05.04)  
 
7.  Infer causes of problems or of outcomes that reflect life through 
literature, including literature recognized for quality and literary merit. 
(R. NT. 05.01) 
 
 
 
“I Ca n” Statements  
 
 
1.  I can make predictions based on life experiences  
             and other things I have read, heard, or observed. 
 
 
 
2.  I can express my understanding of characters, events, and 
themes through my personal experiences through discussion 
and writing. 
 
3.  I can identify the theme of a story and the common theme 
when comparing two stories. 
 
 
4.  I can think about what I know before reading and adjust 
this knowledge as I read new information. 
 
5.  I can build upon my knowledge as I read new information. 
 
 
6.  I can use a character chart to understand how characters in 
my book change and grow. 
 
 
 
7.  I can figure out why outcomes occur based on text to text, 
text to self, or text to world connections.

Comprehension -Thinking about the text  
Students will… 
 
1.  Understand when a writer has combined underlying organizational 
structures (description, compare and contrast, temporal sequence, 
problem and solution, cause and effect). (R.IT.05.02) (R.MT.05.02) 
 
 
 
 
2.  Notice and understand figurative and descriptive language and the role it 
plays in enhancing a text. (R.NT.05.04) 
 
3.  Notice and reflect on a writer’s use of idioms.  
           (R.NT.05.04) 
 
4.  Notice how an author uses words in a connotative way 
     (to imply something beyond the literal meaning).       
     (R.NT.05.04) 
 
5.  Analyze the structure, elements, style, and purpose of narrative genre 
including historical fiction, tale tales, science fiction, fantasy, and 
mystery. (R.NT.05.02) 
 
 
 
 
6.  Identify and describe informational text patterns including compare and 
contrast, cause and effect, and problem/solution. (R.IT.05.02) 
 
 
7.  Evaluate the text in terms of readers’ own experience. (R.CS.05.01) 
 
 
 
“I Can” Statements  
 
 
1.  I can determine and understand when an author has used: 
a.  description 
b.  compare and contrast 
c.  sequencing 
d.  problem/solution 
e.  cause/effect 
 
2-4. I can understand how an author uses idioms, similes, and 
metaphors to enhance meaning of the story. 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
5.  I can identify the forms and features of  
a.  historical fiction 
b.  tall tales 
c.  science fiction 
d.  fantasy 
e.  mystery 
 
6.  see #1 above 
 
 
 
7.  I can evaluate a story using my personal experiences.

Test Taking Genre  
Students will… 
 
1.  Students will respond to a prompt for paired reading selections. 
(R.CM.05.03) 
 
2.  Students will answer cross-text questions for paired reading selections. 
(R.CM.05.03) 
 
3.  Respond to individual and multiple texts by finding evidence, discussing, 
illustrating, and or writing to reflect, make connections, take a position, 
and or show understanding. (R.CM.05.03) 
 
4.  Students will use testing strategies (Make a prediction form titles and 
pictures, make a prediction from highlighted key words in questions, use 
reading strategies while reading a selection, show evidence of “search 
and find” and “right there” and “author and me answers”, process of 
elimination). (R.MT. 05.01) 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
“I Can” Statements  
 
 
1.     I can write to a prompt about two texts. 
 
 
2.     I can answer questions that compare and contrast two    
      paired texts. 
 
3.     I can answer a prompt or question that refers to one text    
      or more than one text. 
 
 
4.     I can use test-taking strategies to answer questions.

DRAFT DRAFT DRAFT DRAFT  Reader Profile for Grade Five (Level U/50) DRAFT DRAFT DRAFT DRAFT  
8/18/09  Troy School District Reading Committee                                                               7 
Word Study  
Students will… 
 
1.  Read words with a full range of plurals including irregular plurals. 
 
2.  Add a variety of endings to words (-able, -ible, -ent, -ant) and discuss 
changes in spelling and meaning. 
 
3.  Work flexibly with base words, making new words by changing letters 
and adding prefixes and suffixes. 
 
4.  Recognize words that have multiple meanings (a form of homograph: bay, 
bay) homographs (look the same, sound different: contract, contract), 
and homophones (sound the same, look different: flair, flare). 
 
5.  Read and derive meaning of words that are related to each other 
because they have the same base or root word (monarch, monarchs, 
monarchy). (RWS.05.02) 
 
6.  Recognize words with frequently appearing vowel and syllable patterns 
(ic(k) in dicker, organic; -ble in implausible, stable). 
 
7.  Read words with consonant clusters and long and short vowel patterns, 
including vowel patterns with r, that appear in multi-syllable words. 
“I Can” Statements  
 
 
1.  I can read words that are plurals. 
 
2.  I can add a variety of endings to words and talk about the 
changes in spelling and meaning. 
 
3.  I can work with base words and add prefixes and suffixes. 
 
 
4.  I can recognize that some words have multiple meanings, or 
are homographs or homophones. 
 
 
5.  I can read and understand words and how they are related 
by looking at their base or root word. 
 
 
6.  I can recognize words with common word patterns. 
 
 
7.  I can read multi-syllable words with consonant clusters and 
vowel patterns.

Essential Classroom Components for Effective Reading Instruction  
Context for Instruction :   
 Room environment : 
/checkbld Room arrangement allows for whole group instruction 
/checkbld Room arrangement allows for small group instruction 
/checkbld Room arrangement allows for  student independent rea ding 
/checkbld Students have access to materials and books 
 Resources : 
/checkbld Classroom has an organized independent library (for e xample, includes level and/or  
genre/author of books) 
/checkbld Class constructed reading charts are current and clearly d isplayed for student reference 
/checkbld Reading response logs (journals/binders) are available for each student 
/checkbld Guided reading/shared reading materials are accessible f or lesson use 
/checkbld Making Meaning  is utilized  
 
Instructional  Approach :  Time is allotted for  
 
 Shared reading    Note:  Shared reading is more prevalent in the reading instructional block in the Kdg – 2nd  
grade classrooms.  In the upper grades, shared reading is more common in the other curricular areas.  
/checkbld Teacher utilizes appropriate  text to model specific instructional skill/strategy that 
students need  
/checkbld Book introduction sets the purpose and teacher model ing helps students construct 
meaning  
/checkbld Instruction is explicit and is focused on a grade appropr iate strategy 
 
 Read Aloud - Making Meaning and other appropriate teacher selected text  
Before : 
/checkbld Teacher gives a brief overview of the book  
/checkbld Teacher invites predictions about the book  
/checkbld Teacher sets purpose for listening  
During : 
/checkbld Teacher provides opportunities for students to develop  concepts and vocabulary 
/checkbld Teacher models constructing meaning  
/checkbld Teacher uses appropriate questions to guide comprehensio n  
/checkbld Teacher provides a model of fluent and expressive read ing  
After : 
/checkbld Teacher and students discuss meaning of the story  
/checkbld Teacher and students confirm or disconfirm predictions  
 
 
210
2 
 
 Reading Workshop:  
Strategy Based  Mini-Lesson : 
/checkbld Lesson is focused on one clear strategy or procedure 
/checkbld Teacher introduces the strategy or procedure with clear,  concrete examples 
/checkbld Teacher models, or demonstrates how  and when  to apply the strategy or procedure 
Active Engagement :  
/checkbld Students have an opportunity to engage in the strate gy or procedure during the 
lesson  
/checkbld Students will practice the strategy or procedure during  guided reading and/or 
independent reading 
Independent Reading (IDR)  
/checkbld Students have appropriate level books for use during I DR in book bags/bins 
/checkbld Students follow clearly established routines and proce dures 
/checkbld Assignments reinforce previous learning 
/checkbld Assignments provide practice at the student’s independen t level 
/checkbld Students are engaged in authentic reading/writing ac tivities that reflect the 
instruction 
/checkbld Students are on-task and working responsibly 
/checkbld Teacher utilizes this time to conference  with individual students (individual 
student goals/progress is noted)  and/or… 
/checkbld Teacher utilizes this time to assess  individual student progress  and/or… 
/checkbld Teacher utilizes this time to work with small groups of students (guided 
reading/strategy/book club) 
• Guided Reading 
: 
o Book selection matches student instructional level, with  a few challenging 
features 
o Book introduction provides appropriate support for t he students according to 
their level, strengths and needs. 
o Teacher closely observes and notes students’ reading beh avior. 
o Teacher prompts students and provides feedback 
o Teacher and students discuss comprehension of story 
• Strategy Group  
o Intervention is directly linked to classroom instruction a nd learner’s identified 
needs  
o Teacher provides clear explanation of task  
o Teacher demonstrates using clear examples to connect  to prior knowledge  
o Teacher leads guided practice providing explicit feedba ck to scaffold learner  
o Student independently practices skill or strategy  
• Guided Literature Discussion (Book Club)  
o Students have already read the selection  
o Teacher facilitates a focused conversation about the te xt  
o Most of conversation is generated and produced by stu dents  
o When necessary, teacher prompts students’ use of comprehe nsion strategies

o Teacher provides specific feedback to students about strate gy use

/checkbld As the students are working  during  IDR, a student tha t is demonstrating the task is 
chosen to share with the class   
Lesson Closing : 
/checkbld Teacher selects a student who applied mini-lesson to shar e their work with the 
class 
/checkbld Teacher leads a conversation to provide feedback on the new learning and to 
reinforce desired behaviors 
 
 Anchor charts solidify and support students’ learning of the skills/strategies 
 
 Displays of student work  support learning objectives

B.  REPORT:  Middle School Health Textbook 
Recommendation 
 
During the fall of 2009, middle school health teachers 
met to review health materials from various textbook 
publishers.  Once the selection was made, the 
materials on human sexuality, HIV, and AIDS were 
presented for review and approval by the Sex 
Education Advisory Board on November 9, 2009.  
Tonight, we will be presenting the committee’s 
recommendation for the new middle school health 
program. 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
RECOMMENDED ACTION:  This evening’s report will be 
given by Alicia Wahls, health teacher from Smith Middle 
School, and Larry Hahn, principal of Baker Middle School, 
who is responsible for the middle school health curriculum.

 Textbook Recommendation  
For the 2010-2011 School Year 
 
 
Middle School Health  
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
Textbook Review Committee Members:  
Sean Fisher 
Michael Hosbach 
Andrea Lothian 
Judy Steinberger 
Alicia Wahls 
 
 
214
Middle School Health  
 
 
Rationale for Textbook Search  
• Part of a pre-determined rotation schedule. 
• Current textbook has a copyright of 2001 and was due to be reviewed.  
• The current textbook has outdated information that is a large part of our 
curriculum.  
 
 
Textbook Selection Process  
The following books were previewed for Health:  
• Teen Health 
, (Course 1-3), Glencoe Publishing, copyright 2009  
• Decision for Health , Holt McDougal Publishing, copyright 2008  
• Health and Wellness , MacMillan Publishing, copyright 2009  
 
 
The Following Criteria Were Used in the Selection Process:  
• Components:  Layout, Organization, Teacher Accessibility  
• Content – Student Accessibility  
• Research Based Instructional Strategies, Best Practice 
• Accuracy, Reliability 
• GLCEs for middle school covered 
• Readability 
 
 
Features of the Recommended Text:  
 
Components: Layout, Organization  - Teacher Accessibility  
The content and organization of the program contribute to ease of use.  
The supplemental materials are organized and easy to access.  
The Teacher’s Edition is well organized and easy to use.  
The text and teacher materials are easily accessible electronically and are NIMAS 
compliant. 
The program links to interactive technology tools and digital resources (media 
clips, etc.). 
Ample supp lemental resources at various levels facilitate differentiation within a 
classroom. 
The program provides adequate review and extension activities to supplement 
instruction. 
Explicit SmartBoard applications are available . 
Ongoing, formative assessments are provided at regular intervals.  
Data from assessments is diagnostic and informs instruction.  
 
  
215
Content - Student Accessibility  
 
The material is suitable to the maturity level of students.  
Text material is written in rich, natural language. 
The photographs, graphics, and illustrations help the students understand the 
written text. 
The vocabulary is both challenging and appropriate. 
Text features and structures enhance student learning.  
The selections are of interest and engaging to the students.  
The content fosters creative activities for extending knowledge in a variety of 
ways including drama, writing, movement, music, etc. 
The content engages students to become actively involved.  
The supplemental materials facilitate, enhance, and extend learning.  
The materials provide students with necessary historical, social , and political 
background knowledge. 
 
Research Based Instructional Strategies, Best Practice  
 
The materials provide opportunities for students to think critically.  
Instructional practices are current, credible, and cited.  
Content is consistent with findings in research in learning theory and child 
development. 
Concepts and skills are developed in a continuous and expanding progression.  
 
Accuracy, Reliability  
 
The factual information is accurate.  
Reading levels (or L exiles ) are grade level appropriate for the course.  
Middle School GLCEs for g rades 6 -7. 
The GLCE alignment is clearly indicated.  
 
 
Textbook Recommendation for Middle School Health:  
 
The chapters containing human sexuality and HIV/AIDS information was 
presented for review and approved by the Troy School District Sex Education 
Advisory Board on November 9, 2009.   
 
The textbook review committee unanimously recommends the adoption of 
Teen 
Health  , Course 3; written by Bronson, Cleary, Hubbard, © 2009 published by 
Glencoe for use in the Troy School District beginning in the 2010-2011 school 
year.

 DATE:  March 10, 2010 
  
 TO: Michael Adamczyk 
  Assistant Superintendent 
  Business Services 
   
 FROM: Frank E. Lams 
  Supervisor, Purchasing and Budget 
 
          RE:      Bid Award #9648 
                      Athens Media Center Furniture                      
 
 
 
On January 12, 2010 bids were opened for replacing the furniture in the media center at 
Athens High School.  Bids were sent to 16 potential bidders with eight responding (none 
of which were “No Bid”). 
     
Samples of the proposed shelving, tables and chairs were obtained from the lowest five 
complete bidders. Dr.. R. Machesky, Assistant Superintendent for Secondary 
Education, Ms. L. Dixon, Athens High School Principal, G. Bell, District Carpenter, 
yourself and I all met on two separate occasions to review these samples and the 
related bid submissions.    
 
After extensive discussions, the above group decided that the best value – considering 
cost, construction quality and style – would be to split the award between bidders as 
follows: 
 
 Office Express: Media Seating, Miscellaneous Seating 
 3 rd  Coast: Shelving, Tables, Custom Counter (Alt. #1) 
  Undecided: Lounge Furniture; A recommendation for t his will be taken to the 
Board of Education at the April 20 th  meeting. 
 
Accordingly, I recommend award of this bid as follows: 
  
Office Express  $ 16,957.18 
3rd  Coast     66,703.75 
 
                            $ 83,660.93  
 
Note that the recommendation for media seating ($13,374.93) is the overall low bid for 
that category, the recommendation for misc. seating ($3,582.25) is the lowest bid to 
specification and the recommendation for shelving, tables and the custom computer 
counter ($66,703.75) is the overall second lowest bidder for the combination of those 
furniture categories. 
 
Please include this as an action item on the March 16, 2010 Board agenda as an 
expenditure of the 2004 Capital Projects Fund.

 DATE:  March 11, 2010

 FROM: Frank E. Lams 
  Supervisor, Purchasing and Budget 
 
          RE:      Bid Award #9661 
                      Bus Security Systems                      
 
 
 
On February 24, 2010 bids were opened for the purchase of additional security systems 
(surveillance cameras) for installation inside the District’s school buses.   Bids were sent 
to 12 potential bidders with five responding (none of which were “No Bid”).   
 
Results of this bid have been reviewed with Mr. D. Asaro, Transportation Supervisor, 
and Ms. A. Johnson, Contract Manager-First Student, who have recommended award 
to Unity School Bus Parts.  Please note that Unity’s bid was for a Seon camera system 
which is the same system being used in some of our buses currently.   I concur with this 
recommendation.    
 
Working within a budgetary constraint of $50,000.00, I recommend the following: 
 
 Purchase 20 Complete Systems  $ 29,600.00 
     Purchase 41 Decoy Systems  20,295.00 
 
  
 Total Purchase from Unity Bus Parts  $ 49,895.00 
  
 
This purchase will allow all of our non-spare buses to be equipped with a camera 
system mount.  The twenty cameras (along with the existing five) can be switched 
between any bus in the fleet allowing greater flexibility and usage.  
  
Please include this as an action item on the March 16, 2010 Board agenda as an 
expenditure of the 2004 Capital Projects Fund.

 New Board Policy D-1106, Fixed Assets

Act, and the Revised School Code.

Policy, D-1106, Fixed Assets, is presented at this evening’s 
meeting.

257
  MEETINGS OF THE BOARD     B-1200 
 
 
B-1208 VOTING 
 
 
The Board of Education shall conduct all of its votes in   
 accordance with the applicable federal and state laws including , 
but not limited to, the Revised School Code and the Open 
Meetings Act, as amended. 
 
The roll call for voting is by random call of last names on a 
rotating basis. 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
Adopted: January 16, 1979 
First Reading: March 16, 2010 
  Pending adoption on April 20, 2010  
258
MEETINGS OF THE BOARD     B-1200  
 
 
B-1211 PUBLIC PARTICIPATION IN BOARD OF EDUCATION 
MEETINGS 
 
 
All meetings, except closed sessions, are open to the public.  The Board 
welcomes citizens’ comments.  The public participation portion of the 
meeting is limited to one -half hour , but exceptions may be made at the 
discretion of the Board president.  Each p erson will be allowed to 
speak for up to five minutes, except where the number of speakers 
exceeds the time limit.  In those instances, the President may reduce 
the five -minute limit to a three -minute limit for each speaker  or the 
president of the Board ma y waive the half –hour time limit.  In any 
event, no one’s right to address the Board will be denied. 
 
Each person wishing to address the Board of Education must identify 
himself or herself by name and address.  If the person is representing 
an organizatio n or group, the person should indicate whether the 
comments or presentation represents the official view of the 
organization or group. 
 
Board of Education members may question speakers but are not 
obligated to answer questions or make statements or commitm ents in 
response to issues raised by the public.  In general, such issues will be 
referred to the Superintendent for investigation, study, and 
recommendation or designated as future agenda items for Board of 
Education consideration. 
 
Individuals addressing 
 the Board should take into consideration the 
rules of common courtesy.  The public participation portion of the 
meeting cannot be used to make personal attacks against any 
individual school board member or school district employee that are 
totally unrelat ed to the manner in which the member or employee 
performs his or her duties. 
 
To ensure individual due process rights, the District maintains a 
formal process for handling complaints against individuals.  A 
problem involving an individual or specific incid ent is handled 
through administrative channels.  For assistance, contact the 
Superintendent’s office. 
 
The president or designee is responsible for the orderly conduct of the 
meeting and shall rule on such matters as to the appropriateness of the 
subject b eing presented.  
259
MEETINGS OF THE BOARD     B-1200  
 
 
B-1211 PUBLIC PARTICIPATION IN BOARD OF EDUCATION 
MEETINGS 
 
 
The Board values public participation and recognizes the importance 
of public input on issues affecting the Board and the District.  This 
Policy has been adopted to balance the importance of pub lic 
participation with other important objectives, including: insuring all 
community members a fair and reasonable opportunity to express 
their views to the Board; completing Board business effectively, 
efficiently and in an orderly manner; respecting other important time 
commitments of Board members, District staff and audience members; 
and, respecting the rights of Board members, District staff and 
students and audience members. 
Public participation will be limited to 60 minutes per Board meeting.  
Generally, there will be 30 minutes of public participation at the 
beginning of the meeting, limited to agenda items.  There will be an 
additional 30 minutes of public participation at the end of the meeting, 
limited to non-agenda items. 
Although the public participation portion of the meeting is limited, an 
exception may be made, if necessary, so that no one’s right to address 
the Board will be denied. 
Audience members who wish to address the Board must complete a 
Speaker Request Form.  Forms will be available 30 minutes prior to the 
start of the meeting and must be completed and submitted to the 
Secretary of the Board of Education before the start of the meeting.  
The Board President or presiding officer will recognize audience 
members for public participation subject to the following rules: 
• Each person wishing to speak must identify self by name and 
address. 
• Recognized speakers may be permitted to speak for up to three (3) 
minutes.  However, the President of the Board may reduce the time 
limit for each speaker in order to accommodate all of the 
individuals who wish to address the Board of Education. 
• Recognized speakers may not transfer or delegate their time to 
others. 
260
MEETINGS OF THE BOARD     B-1200  
 
 
B-1211 PUBLIC PARTICIPATION IN BOARD OF EDUCATION 
MEETINGS 
 
 
• Delegations who wish to express their views are encouraged to 
 select one speaker to present the delegation’s views to the  Board. 
• Each member of a delegation wishing to speak must submit a 
 separate Speaker Request form. 
• Persons who have completed Speaker Request forms but have not 
had any opportunity to address the Board or to complete their 
presentations may be given the option of submitting their views in 
writing to the Secretary of the Board of Education, whose name and 
e-mail address are printed on each Speaker Request form.  The 
Secretary of the Board will forward all such written presentation to 
all Board members. 
• Speakers shall direct their comments to the Board and not to 
District staff or other audience members. 
• Speakers should not expect Board members to answer questions 
during public participation.  Questions will be referred to the 
Superintendent for review and study and, if appropriate, future 
Board action. 
• Speakers are expected to express themselves responsibly and 
courteously and with due respect for the fact their views and 
opinions may not be shared by all present.  The following are 
examples of irresponsible or discourteous expression:  
/head2right Denigrating Board members, District staff or students or other 
audience members. 
/head2right Speculating on the motives of Board members, District staff or 
students or other audience members. 
/head2right Complaints about District staff or students that have not been 
brought to the District’s attention through appropriate channels. 
• Speakers may not express complaints against Board members or 
District staff unrelated to their offices or jobs. 
261
MEETINGS OF THE BOARD     B-1200  
 
 
B-1211 PUBLIC PARTICIPATION IN BOARD OF EDUCATION 
MEETINGS 
 
 
• Speakers may not express complaints against Board members or 
District staff that are related to their offices or job unless the 
affected person or persons have been notified and permitted a 
reasonable opportunity to choose whether to hold a hearing on the 
complaint in open or closed session. 
• Audience members are expected to conduct themselves responsibly 
and courteously and with due respect for the fact their views and 
opinions may not be shared by all present.  Thus, raucous or 
disrespectful expressions of agreement or disagreement by 
audience members are not appropriate. 
• The Board’s President or presiding officer is charged with the 
responsibility for insuring that public participation assists the 
Board in discharging its responsibilities and is conducted according 
to this Policy.  Thus, the Board President or presiding officer is 
authorized to enforce this Policy by, among other things: 
interrupting presentations to remind speakers and audience 
members of this Policy; dismissing previously recognized speakers 
who violate this Policy; requesting speakers or audience members 
to leave the meeting if they violate the Policy and, if necessary, 
requesting the assistance of law enforcement officers to assist in 
removing speakers or audience members who refuse to leave; or, 
adjourning the meeting. 
The Board may amend or waive this Policy, or specific provisions 
thereof, when, in the Board’s judgment, amendment or waiver is 
appropriate. 
Revised: April 1988 
 March 17, 1998 
 July 10, 2007 
 March 17, 2009 
First Rdg: March 16, 2010 
 Pending adoption on April 20, 2010

   RULES AND REGULATIONS    C-1400 
 
 
 
C-1402 SMOKING 
 
The policy of the Troy School District is to follow Public Act 140 of 
1993 regarding smoking and use of tobacco products on school 
district property, with the exception that smoking is prohibited at 
ath letic fields. 
 
It is the policy of the Troy School District that the use and/or sale of 
any tobacco products is prohibited in vehicles, buildings, facilities, 
structures, or other real estate owned, leased or otherwise 
controlled by the Troy School District at all times (24 hours a day – 
7 days a week.) 
 
For the purpose of this policy, the use and/or sale of a tobacco 
product is defined as follows: 
 
“Tobacco product” means a preparation of tobacco to be inhaled, 
chewed, or placed in a person’s mouth. 
 
“Use of a tobacco product” means any of the following: 
 
/head2right The carrying by a person of a lighted cigar, cigarette, pipe, or 
 other lighted smoking device. 
 
/head2right The inhaling or chewing of a tobacco product. 
 
/head2right The placing of a tobacco product within a person’s mouth. 
 
 
 
 
 
Adopted: February 2, 1999 
Revised: September, 2003 
First Rdg: March 16, 2010 
  Pending adoption on April 20, 2010  
264
 
 
   ACCOUNTING AND REPORTING     D-1100 
 
 
 
D-1106 FIXED ASSET POLICY  
 
The Board shall maintain a fixed asset procedure sufficient to 
track applicable purchases and donations of items meeting the 
following criteria: 
 
• The cost of each item shall exceed $5,000;  
 
• The item shall have an estimated useful life which exceeds 
one year; 
 
• Items costing less than $5,000 each may also be included 
within this policy if such item is deemed to be theft prone or 
otherwise warranting tracking; 
 
Fixed assets shall be classified into the following major 
categories: 
 
/head2right Land 
/head2right Buildings  
/head2right Improvements other than buildings 
/head2right Construction in Progress 
/head2right Machinery and Equipment 
 
The Assistant Superintendent of Business Services shall develop 
such procedures and practices sufficient to meet this policy. 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
First Rdg: March 16, 2010 
  Pending adoption on April 20, 2010 
 
265
 
 
ADVERTISING & DISTRIBUTION OF MATERIALS IN THE SCHOOLS 
K-1300   
 
 
 
K-1303 POLITICAL 
AND/OR COMMERCIAL  ADVERTISING BY 
DISTRICT EMPLOYEES  
 
 No material supporting any candidate, candidates, slate of candidates, 
political issues, issues which cause substantial and material interference 
and/or disruption with normal school activities , or political 
organizations/committees may be distributed, posted, or displayed on 
any district-owned property. 
 
/head2right Except for the reserved length of time in a building or room which 
has been rented or reserved by a political or community group for 
meeting purposes or in connection with a class project or activities. 
 
All school personnel, including contracted personnel assigned to the  
school district, shall maintain a posture of complete neutrality with 
respect to all candidate(s), political issues, and/or political 
organizations/committees while engaged in duties for the Troy School 
District or in contact with students or students’ parents. 
 
Further, absent the express written consent of the superintendent or 
his/her designee, all school personnel shall be restricted from utilizing 
Troy School District resources, technology, including District e-mail, and 
student/parent contact information received while engaged in duties for 
the Troy School District for commercial purposes or for the purpose of 
supporting any candidate, candidates, slate of candidates, political 
issues, including any issues which cause substantial and material 
interference and/or disruption with normal school activities, or political 
organizations/committees. 
 
Adopted: May 3, 1976 
Revised: January 16, 1979 
 April 21, 1998 
First Rdg: March 16, 2010 
 For adoption on April 20, 2010 
266
 
 
ADVERTISING & DISTRIBUTION OF MATERIALS IN THE SCHOOLS 
K-1300   
 
K-1304 SERVICE ADVERTISING 
 
 
 Advertising of a service to employees or service to students may be allowed by 
obtaining approval of the superintendent or designee.  
 
 ADVERTISING ON SCHOOL DISTRICT PROPERTY AND IN SCHOOL 
DISTRICT ELECTRONIC MEDIA  
 
The school district property and electronic media, like school newspapers, 
yearbooks, and other District-sponsored publications and property, is a nonpublic 
forum subject to the District’s control and regulation.  The District will accept 
commercial advertisements, subject to the following rules and procedures. 
 
• Advertising is restricted to commercial purposes.  (The District reserves the 
right to give preference to local businesses and businesses that sell goods and 
services that students may be interested in purchasing.)  The District property 
and Web site are not forums for political, religious, or public advocacy 
discussion or expression. 
 
• The District reserves the right to deny advertisements that are ungrammatical, 
poorly written, inadequately researched, biased or prejudiced, vulgar or 
profane or otherwise unsuitable for student audiences. 
 
• Advertisements cannot be for goods or services which are illegal. 
 
• The sponsors, goods, services, advertisement or activities may not violate or 
condone (explicitly or implicitly) the violation of Board or School District’s 
policies, or be inconsistent with or in competition with the educational mission 
of the School District. 
 
• The advertisements cannot be libelous, offensive, contain explicit sexual 
content, or be obscene. 
 
• The District will not approve advertising in connection with the following 
products or uses: 
 
1.  Products used to injure or kill. 
2.  Alcohol-related products. 
3.  Tobacco-related products. 
4.  Illegal drug-related products. 
267
 
 
5.  Prescription or over-the-counter medication. 
6.  Sexually suggestive products or language. 
7.  Gambling-related products. 
8.  Products that present an unacceptable risk of liability. 
9.  Products that are harmful to or inconsistent with the mission or image of 
the School District. 
10.  Racist, sexist, hateful, demeaning or degrading language or statements. 
11.  Profanity. 
12.  Sexual acts. 
13.  Statements impugning any school in the Troy School District or schools 
outside of the Troy School District. 
14.  Art or a design incorporating trademarks or copyrights owned by third 
parties, unless written permission of such use is satisfactory in form and 
substance to the school, is obtained from the trademark holder or 
copyright owner, and approved in advance. 
15.  Any use which suggests or implies the school district’s endorsement of 
other organizations, companies, products, services, political parties or 
views, or religious organizations or beliefs. 
16.  Any use that discriminates or implies discrimination against any persons 
or groups based on age, ancestry, belief, color, creed, disability, national 
origin, race, ethnicity, religion, sex, sexual orientation or veteran status, or 
in any other way that would be a violation of the School District’s anti-
discrimination policies or practices. 
 
All advertisements are to be reviewed by the superintendent or designee who is 
responsible for applying and enforcing this policy.  Any entity or person who 
disagrees with the superintendent or designee’s decision can petition the 
superintendent’s office for a review within 30 calendar days from the initial 
written decision of the designee.  The superintendent, or the superintendent’s 
designee, will review the petition and upon request meet with the petitioner.  The 
superintendent or designee will issue a decision within 30 calendar days after the 
petition and/or meeting which decision shall be final. 
 
 
Adopted: April 19, 1976

10. CLOSED SESSION 
 
 A. CONSIDERATION:  Negotiations 
 
The Board must pass a resolution to go into closed session. 
Following the passage of the resolution, the Board of 
Education will go into closed session.  No additional business 
will take place after this time, other than to officially adjourn 
the meeting.
